The reason I’m here is really that at work, with all those interruptions, I lose my train of thought constantly, and it’s been really frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble staying focused when you're doing quiet activities alone?\n\nPatient: I know you’re asking specifically about quiet activities alone, and I’m trying to answer that directly, but I keep coming back to the same thing because that’s honestly what feels like the problem I can actually describe clearly.\n\nThe main reason I’m here is that on the floor, I lose my train of thought constantly. I’ll be holding a patient flow issue in my head — like what I was about to follow up on, who needed something next, what step I was in the middle of — and then something pretty normal happens, like an IV pump beeping, or someone asks another nurse a question nearby, or a call light goes off. And suddenly it’s just gone. Like I’ve forgotten what I was working on, and I have to stand there and mentally rewind.\n\nSo for quiet activities alone… I don’t have as clear of an example right now. It’s really the work situation, with all the interruptions, that made me feel like, okay, this is happening constantly and it’s becoming a real problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience difficulty staying focused or losing your train of thought when you are alone at home?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I’m still having a hard time giving you a clean answer about being alone at home. I don’t want to say something just to make it fit if that’s not really what stands out to me.\n\nWhat I can say is the main thing that made me come in is what happens on the floor. That’s where I notice it constantly. I’ll be trying to hold a patient flow issue in my head—like who I was about to check on, what needed follow-up, what step I was in the middle of—and then an IV pump beeps, or someone nearby asks another nurse a question, or a call light goes off, and it’s like the whole thread just disappears. I’ll literally stop and think, “Wait, what was I just doing?” and then I have to reconstruct it.\n\nSo at home alone… I’m not saying it never happens, I just don’t have a clear example the way I do at work. The work interruptions are the part that feels constant and really disruptive.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have trouble staying focused if you were doing a complex task at home without any interruptions?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would, actually. Maybe not in the same way as when there are constant interruptions, but if the task itself requires a lot of sustained focus, I really struggle to even get myself to start it.\n\nLike, if I know something is going to involve sitting down and doing a detailed chart review—say auditing documentation across shifts or going back through notes carefully and comparing things—I’ll keep putting it off. I’ll tell myself, “Okay, I need to do that,” and then somehow I’m suddenly doing every bedside task I can justify doing first. Checking on something, restocking something, answering something, tidying up, anything that feels more immediate and concrete.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t understand the task. It’s more like sitting down with it feels like trying to climb a wall. I can feel this resistance before I even start, because I know it’s going to take that steady mental effort and I’m going to have to hold all these details in my head. So if I were at home with a complex task like that, even without interruptions, I think the difficulty would still be there—especially if it was the kind of thing that takes prolonged, focused review.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be more able to focus on a complex task at home if it was something you were really interested in?\n\nPatient: Maybe somewhat, but I don’t think interest completely fixes it for me. Like, if something is genuinely engaging, I might have an easier time getting into it, at least at first. But the part I keep noticing is when I know a task is going to take that heavy, sustained mental effort — especially something like focused chart review or auditing documentation across shifts — I start avoiding it.\n\nIt’s like I can feel the weight of it before I even begin. I’ll think, “Okay, I need to sit down and go through this carefully,” and then I’ll suddenly find ten bedside tasks that feel more concrete or urgent. I’ll check on something, organize something, follow up on something smaller, because those feel easier to move through. But actually sitting down and holding all those details in my head feels like trying to climb a wall.\n\nSo if it were something I was really interested in at home, I might be more willing to start, but if it still required that same kind of prolonged, detailed focus, I think I’d still struggle and probably push it off.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with staying focused is related to not being motivated or interested in the tasks you're doing?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s just motivation or interest, no. I mean, interest probably helps a little, like if something feels more engaging I can sometimes get myself into it easier. But the problem is bigger than just, “I don’t care about this.”\n\nThe clearest example is when I know something is going to take a lot of sustained focus, like really sitting down to do chart review or auditing documentation across shifts. I know it matters, and I’m not indifferent to it. I can even feel stressed because I know it needs to get done. But I keep pushing it off anyway. I’ll suddenly find all these bedside tasks that feel more immediate or concrete — checking on something, following up on something smaller, organizing something, anything where I can keep moving.\n\nAnd it’s not like I’m choosing fun things instead. That’s the part that makes me think it’s not just motivation. I’m not avoiding work to relax. I’m avoiding the kind of work where I have to sit still mentally and hold a bunch of details in my head for a long stretch. Sitting down with that kind of task feels like trying to climb a wall, even when I know it’s important.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble staying focused when doing tasks in a quiet, private space without interruptions?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually one of the things that makes it frustrating, because I can’t always blame it on noise or people interrupting me.\n\nIf I’m in a quiet room and there’s nothing obviously pulling me away, I still have trouble with tasks that require sustained mental effort. Like if I know I need to sit down and do a focused chart review, or audit documentation across shifts, I’ll keep avoiding it. I’ll tell myself, “Okay, now is the time, I’m alone, it’s quiet, I should be able to just do this,” and then I still somehow find other things to do first.\n\nAt work, that usually looks like me getting up to do bedside tasks instead — checking on something, organizing something, following up on something smaller — because those feel more concrete and easier to start. The chart review feels like this big wall in front of me. It’s not even that I don’t know how to do it. It’s more that sitting down and staying with it, holding all the details in my head and going through them carefully, feels almost impossible to make myself do.\n\nSo yes, even without interruptions, if the task is mentally heavy and detailed, I really struggle to stay focused on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it easier to focus when you are in a familiar environment, like your home, compared to a busy workplace?\n\nPatient: Honestly, the familiar environment helps a little, but the bigger issue for me is how busy and distracting the floor is. That’s actually one of the main reasons I’m here.\n\nAt work, I lose my train of thought constantly. I’ll be in the middle of thinking through some patient flow issue — like what needs to happen next, who still needs something, what I was about to follow up on — and then an IV pump starts beeping, or someone asks another nurse a question near me, or a call light goes off, and it’s like the thought just disappears. I’ll stop and think, “Wait, what was I doing?” or “What was I about to check?” And sometimes I can get it back, but other times I have to retrace everything mentally, which takes time and makes me feel really scattered.\n\nSo compared to that, yes, home can feel easier because there aren’t as many constant alarms and voices and interruptions. But it’s not like being somewhere familiar magically makes my focus solid. It’s more that the workplace has so many competing things pulling at my attention that I’m constantly getting knocked off track.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your difficulty staying focused is the same whether you're doing work tasks or personal tasks?\n\nPatient: It’s not exactly the same, no. I mean, I can get distracted with personal things too, but the place where it’s really obvious — and honestly the main reason I’m here — is at work on the floor.\n\nWhen I’m doing work tasks, especially when I’m trying to keep track of patient flow or what needs to happen next, I lose my train of thought constantly. I’ll be in the middle of thinking, “Okay, this patient needs this, I need to follow up on that, I was about to check—” and then an IV pump starts beeping, or someone nearby asks another nurse a question, or a call light goes off, and suddenly the whole thread is just gone. Like completely gone. I’ll stand there thinking, “Wait, what was I doing?” or “Who was I about to go check on?”\n\nAnd it’s frustrating because it’s not like I’m trying to drift off. I’m actively working, I’m trying to stay on top of things, but the interruptions or even just sounds around me knock me off course so easily. Then I have to backtrack in my head and reconstruct what I was doing, which takes extra time and makes me feel scattered.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say it’s only work tasks, but work is where it causes the biggest problems, because there are so many alarms and voices and things happening all at once.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have the same difficulty staying focused if you were doing your nursing tasks in a quieter, less busy environment?\n\nPatient: I think it would probably be better in a quieter environment, but I don’t know that it would completely go away.\n\nThe main thing that’s making me come in is that on the floor, I lose my train of thought constantly. I’ll be in the middle of sorting out some patient flow issue in my head — like who needs what next, what I was about to follow up on, what step I was going to take — and then an IV pump beeps, or someone nearby asks another nurse a question, or a call light goes off, and it’s like the whole thought just disappears. I’m left standing there thinking, “Wait, what was I just doing?” or “Which patient was I thinking about?”\n\nSo if the environment were quieter and less busy, I do think fewer things would be knocking me off track. But the reason it feels so bad at work is that there are just constant little interruptions and sounds, and each one can completely derail me. It’s not even always someone directly interrupting me — sometimes it’s just something happening nearby, and my brain follows it before I can stop it.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble staying focused when interacting with family or friends?\n\nPatient: With family or friends, I can get distracted sometimes, but it’s honestly not the thing that stands out most to me. The main reason I’m here is really what happens at work, on the floor.\n\nLike, I’ll be in the middle of thinking through a patient flow issue — who needs what next, what I was about to follow up on, what I still need to check — and then an IV pump starts beeping, or someone near me asks another nurse a question, or a call light goes off, and my whole train of thought just drops. Suddenly I’m standing there like, “Wait, what was I doing?” or “Which patient was I just thinking about?” It feels like the thought gets erased.\n\nSo with family or friends, it’s not that I’m never distracted, but it doesn’t feel as disruptive or as obvious as it does at work. On the floor, there are just so many sounds and interruptions that I’m constantly getting pulled away from what I was trying to hold in my head.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

Um… I’m not really sure, honestly. I don’t think of myself as especially fidgety, at least not in the obvious way, like constantly bouncing my leg or needing to get up all the time. I mean, I’m sure I shift around like anyone does if I’m sitting for a long time, but I haven’t really noticed that as a major problem.

When I’m supposed to be sitting still, I don’t usually feel like I physically can’t tolerate it or anything. It’s more that I might get uncomfortable or impatient depending on the situation, but I don’t know if that’s more than normal. No one has really pointed it out to me either, like, “You’re always moving,” or anything like that.

So I guess my answer would be… not often, or at least it’s not something I’ve paid much attention to.

I’ll literally stop and think, “Wait, what was I just doing?” and then I have to reconstruct it.\n\nSo at home alone… I’m not saying it never happens, I just don’t have a clear example the way I do at work. I’ll tell myself, “Okay, I need to do that,” and then somehow I’m suddenly doing every bedside task I can justify doing first. I mean, interest probably helps a little, like if something feels more engaging I can sometimes get myself into it easier. But the problem is bigger than just, “I don’t care about this.”\n\nThe clearest example is when I know something is going to take a lot of sustained focus, like really sitting down to do chart review or auditing documentation across shifts. I’ll suddenly find all these bedside tasks that feel more immediate or concrete — checking on something, following up on something smaller, organizing something, anything where I can keep moving.\n\nAnd it’s not like I’m choosing fun things instead. Sitting down with that kind of task feels like trying to climb a wall, even when I know it’s important.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble staying focused when doing tasks in a quiet, private space without interruptions?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually one of the things that makes it frustrating, because I can’t always blame it on noise or people interrupting me.\n\nIf I’m in a quiet room and there’s nothing obviously pulling me away, I still have trouble with tasks that require sustained mental effort. Like if I know I need to sit down and do a focused chart review, or audit documentation across shifts, I’ll keep avoiding it. I’ll tell myself, “Okay, now is the time, I’m alone, it’s quiet, I should be able to just do this,” and then I still somehow find other things to do first.\n\nAt work, that usually looks like me getting up to do bedside tasks instead — checking on something, organizing something, following up on something smaller — because those feel more concrete and easier to start. The chart review feels like this big wall in front of me. It’s not even that I don’t know how to do it. It’s more that sitting down and staying with it, holding all the details in my head and going through them carefully, feels almost impossible to make myself do.\n\nSo yes, even without interruptions, if the task is mentally heavy and detailed, I really struggle to stay focused on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it easier to focus when you are in a familiar environment, like your home, compared to a busy workplace?\n\nPatient: Honestly, the familiar environment helps a little, but the bigger issue for me is how busy and distracting the floor is. I’ll be in the middle of thinking through some patient flow issue — like what needs to happen next, who still needs something, what I was about to follow up on — and then an IV pump starts beeping, or someone asks another nurse a question near me, or a call light goes off, and it’s like the thought just disappears. But it’s not like being somewhere familiar magically makes my focus solid. It’s more that the workplace has so many competing things pulling at my attention that I’m constantly getting knocked off track.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your difficulty staying focused is the same whether you're doing work tasks or personal tasks?\n\nPatient: It’s not exactly the same, no. That’s where it feels almost impossible to ignore.\n\nLike on the floor, I’ll be in the middle of working through a patient flow issue in my head — who I was going to check on next, what needed follow-up, what I was about to ask someone — and then an IV pump starts beeping, or someone asks another nurse a question near me, or a call light goes off. And it’s like my brain just drops the whole thread. I’ll suddenly be standing there thinking, “Wait… what was I doing?” and I have to backtrack through the last few minutes to reconstruct it.\n\nSo with things I enjoy in my free time… I’m not sure I can give you as clear of an answer right now. It’s not what made me come in. The reason I’m here is really that at work, with all those interruptions, I lose my train of thought constantly, and it’s been really frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble staying focused when you're doing quiet activities alone?\n\nPatient: I know you’re asking specifically about quiet activities alone, and I’m trying to answer that directly, but I keep coming back to the same thing because that’s honestly what feels like the problem I can actually describe clearly.\n\nThe main reason I’m here is that on the floor, I lose my train of thought constantly. I’ll be holding a patient flow issue in my head — like what I was about to follow up on, who needed something next, what step I was in the middle of — and then something pretty normal happens, like an IV pump beeping, or someone asks another nurse a question nearby, or a call light goes off. And suddenly it’s just gone. Like I’ve forgotten what I was working on, and I have to stand there and mentally rewind.\n\nSo for quiet activities alone… I don’t have as clear of an example right now. It’s really the work situation, with all the interruptions, that made me feel like, okay, this is happening constantly and it’s becoming a real problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience difficulty staying focused or losing your train of thought when you are alone at home?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I’m still having a hard time giving you a clean answer about being alone at home. I don’t want to say something just to make it fit if that’s not really what stands out to me.\n\nWhat I can say is the main thing that made me come in is what happens on the floor. That’s where I notice it constantly. I’ll be trying to hold a patient flow issue in my head—like who I was about to check on, what needed follow-up, what step I was in the middle of—and then an IV pump beeps, or someone nearby asks another nurse a question, or a call light goes off, and it’s like the whole thread just disappears. I’ll literally stop and think, “Wait, what was I just doing?” and then I have to reconstruct it.\n\nSo at home alone… I’m not saying it never happens, I just don’t have a clear example the way I do at work. The work interruptions are the part that feels constant and really disruptive.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have trouble staying focused if you were doing a complex task at home without any interruptions?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would, actually. Maybe not in the same way as when there are constant interruptions, but if the task itself requires a lot of sustained focus, I really struggle to even get myself to start it.\n\nLike, if I know something is going to involve sitting down and doing a detailed chart review—say auditing documentation across shifts or going back through notes carefully and comparing things—I’ll keep putting it off. I’ll tell myself, “Okay, I need to do that,” and then somehow I’m suddenly doing every bedside task I can justify doing first. Checking on something, restocking something, answering something, tidying up, anything that feels more immediate and concrete.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t understand the task. It’s more like sitting down with it feels like trying to climb a wall. I can feel this resistance before I even start, because I know it’s going to take that steady mental effort and I’m going to have to hold all these details in my head. So if I were at home with a complex task like that, even without interruptions, I think the difficulty would still be there—especially if it was the kind of thing that takes prolonged, focused review.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be more able to focus on a complex task at home if it was something you were really interested in?\n\nPatient: Maybe somewhat, but I don’t think interest completely fixes it for me. Like, if something is genuinely engaging, I might have an easier time getting into it, at least at first. But the part I keep noticing is when I know a task is going to take that heavy, sustained mental effort — especially something like focused chart review or auditing documentation across shifts — I start avoiding it.\n\nIt’s like I can feel the weight of it before I even begin. I’ll think, “Okay, I need to sit down and go through this carefully,” and then I’ll suddenly find ten bedside tasks that feel more concrete or urgent. I’ll check on something, organize something, follow up on something smaller, because those feel easier to move through. But actually sitting down and holding all those details in my head feels like trying to climb a wall.\n\nSo if it were something I was really interested in at home, I might be more willing to start, but if it still required that same kind of prolonged, detailed focus, I think I’d still struggle and probably push it off.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with staying focused is related to not being motivated or interested in the tasks you're doing?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s just motivation or interest, no. I mean, interest probably helps a little, like if something feels more engaging I can sometimes get myself into it easier. But the problem is bigger than just, “I don’t care about this.”\n\nThe clearest example is when I know something is going to take a lot of sustained focus, like really sitting down to do chart review or auditing documentation across shifts. I know it matters, and I’m not indifferent to it. I can even feel stressed because I know it needs to get done. But I keep pushing it off anyway. I’ll suddenly find all these bedside tasks that feel more immediate or concrete — checking on something, following up on something smaller, organizing something, anything where I can keep moving.\n\nAnd it’s not like I’m choosing fun things instead. That’s the part that makes me think it’s not just motivation. I’m not avoiding work to relax. I’m avoiding the kind of work where I have to sit still mentally and hold a bunch of details in my head for a long stretch. Sitting down with that kind of task feels like trying to climb a wall, even when I know it’s important.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble staying focused when doing tasks in a quiet, private space without interruptions?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually one of the things that makes it frustrating, because I can’t always blame it on noise or people interrupting me.\n\nIf I’m in a quiet room and there’s nothing obviously pulling me away, I still have trouble with tasks that require sustained mental effort. Like if I know I need to sit down and do a focused chart review, or audit documentation across shifts, I’ll keep avoiding it. I’ll tell myself, “Okay, now is the time, I’m alone, it’s quiet, I should be able to just do this,” and then I still somehow find other things to do first.\n\nAt work, that usually looks like me getting up to do bedside tasks instead — checking on something, organizing something, following up on something smaller — because those feel more concrete and easier to start. The chart review feels like this big wall in front of me. It’s not even that I don’t know how to do it. It’s more that sitting down and staying with it, holding all the details in my head and going through them carefully, feels almost impossible to make myself do.\n\nSo yes, even without interruptions, if the task is mentally heavy and detailed, I really struggle to stay focused on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it easier to focus when you are in a familiar environment, like your home, compared to a busy workplace?\n\nPatient: Honestly, the familiar environment helps a little, but the bigger issue for me is how busy and distracting the floor is. That’s actually one of the main reasons I’m here.\n\nAt work, I lose my train of thought constantly. I’ll be in the middle of thinking through some patient flow issue — like what needs to happen next, who still needs something, what I was about to follow up on — and then an IV pump starts beeping, or someone asks another nurse a question near me, or a call light goes off, and it’s like the thought just disappears. I’ll stop and think, “Wait, what was I doing?” or “What was I about to check?” And sometimes I can get it back, but other times I have to retrace everything mentally, which takes time and makes me feel really scattered.\n\nSo compared to that, yes, home can feel easier because there aren’t as many constant alarms and voices and interruptions. But it’s not like being somewhere familiar magically makes my focus solid. It’s more that the workplace has so many competing things pulling at my attention that I’m constantly getting knocked off track.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your difficulty staying focused is the same whether you're doing work tasks or personal tasks?\n\nPatient: It’s not exactly the same, no. I mean, I can get distracted with personal things too, but the place where it’s really obvious — and honestly the main reason I’m here — is at work on the floor.\n\nWhen I’m doing work tasks, especially when I’m trying to keep track of patient flow or what needs to happen next, I lose my train of thought constantly. I’ll be in the middle of thinking, “Okay, this patient needs this, I need to follow up on that, I was about to check—” and then an IV pump starts beeping, or someone nearby asks another nurse a question, or a call light goes off, and suddenly the whole thread is just gone. Like completely gone. I’ll stand there thinking, “Wait, what was I doing?” or “Who was I about to go check on?”\n\nAnd it’s frustrating because it’s not like I’m trying to drift off. I’m actively working, I’m trying to stay on top of things, but the interruptions or even just sounds around me knock me off course so easily. Then I have to backtrack in my head and reconstruct what I was doing, which takes extra time and makes me feel scattered.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say it’s only work tasks, but work is where it causes the biggest problems, because there are so many alarms and voices and things happening all at once.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have the same difficulty staying focused if you were doing your nursing tasks in a quieter, less busy environment?\n\nPatient: I think it would probably be better in a quieter environment, but I don’t know that it would completely go away.\n\nThe main thing that’s making me come in is that on the floor, I lose my train of thought constantly. I’ll be in the middle of sorting out some patient flow issue in my head — like who needs what next, what I was about to follow up on, what step I was going to take — and then an IV pump beeps, or someone nearby asks another nurse a question, or a call light goes off, and it’s like the whole thought just disappears. I’m left standing there thinking, “Wait, what was I just doing?” or “Which patient was I thinking about?”\n\nSo if the environment were quieter and less busy, I do think fewer things would be knocking me off track. But the reason it feels so bad at work is that there are just constant little interruptions and sounds, and each one can completely derail me. The main reason I’m here is really what happens at work, on the floor.\n\nLike, I’ll be in the middle of thinking through a patient flow issue — who needs what next, what I was about to follow up on, what I still need to check — and then an IV pump starts beeping, or someone near me asks another nurse a question, or a call light goes off, and my whole train of thought just drops. Suddenly I’m standing there like, “Wait, what was I doing?” or “Which patient was I just thinking about?” It feels like the thought gets erased.\n\nSo with family or friends, it’s not that I’m never distracted, but it doesn’t feel as disruptive or as obvious as it does at work. I don’t think so, not in a major way. I mean, I’m trying to think of what would count as impulsive. Like, if you mean blurting things out, making risky decisions, spending a bunch of money suddenly, or doing things where afterward I’m like, “Why did I do that?” — that’s not really something I see as a big pattern for me.

I’m pretty aware of consequences, maybe even overly aware sometimes. Especially with work and responsibilities, I tend to think about what could go wrong.

So… no, I wouldn’t say I often act on impulse. At least it’s not one of the problems that stands out to me.

I’m trying really hard, actually, which is part of why it’s so frustrating. I’ll stop and think, “Wait, what was I just doing?” and sometimes I have to backtrack mentally through the last few minutes to remember where I was. It’s happening enough that it’s become the main reason I thought I should come talk to someone, because the floor is already busy and full of interruptions, but I feel like my brain is getting pulled off course way too easily.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing difficulties with focus or staying on track in situations outside of your job as a nurse?\n\nPatient: I mean… the clearest place I notice it is definitely at work, because the floor is just so full of interruptions and consequences, you know? That’s honestly the main reason I came in. I’ll be trying to hold a patient flow issue in my head—like who needs what next, what I was about to follow up on, what’s pending—and then an IV pump beeps, or someone nearby asks another nurse something, or a call light goes off, and it’s like the whole thought just drops out of my brain.\n\nSo outside work… I’m not sure I can describe it as specifically right now, but the work piece is constant and really hard to ignore. It’s that losing-my-train-of-thought thing that’s making me feel like something is off, because I’ll suddenly be standing there thinking, “Wait, what was I doing?” and then I have to kind of retrace my steps mentally. It happens so much on the floor that it’s become really frustrating and embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble staying focused or getting distracted when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: I… honestly, I know you’re asking about outside of work, and I don’t want to dodge the question, but the place it’s most obvious—and the reason I came in—is really the floor. That’s where it feels almost constant.\n\nLike I’ll be in the middle of thinking through a patient flow issue, trying to keep track of what I was about to do next, and then an IV pump beeps, or someone nearby asks another nurse something, or a call light goes off, and suddenly I’ve lost the whole thread. It’s not just that I’m momentarily interrupted—it’s like I genuinely forget what I was working on, and I’m standing there trying to reconstruct it in my head.\n\nOutside of work, I’m not sure I can give as clear an example right now, but at work it happens so often that it’s really what made me feel like I needed help.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any difficulty staying focused when you are doing things you enjoy in your free time?\n\nPatient: Um… I know you’re asking about free time, and I’m trying to think of a good example, but honestly the thing that’s most glaring to me is still work. That’s where it feels almost impossible to ignore.\n\nLike on the floor, I’ll be in the middle of working through a patient flow issue in my head — who I was going to check on next, what needed follow-up, what I was about to ask someone — and then an IV pump starts beeping, or someone asks another nurse a question near me, or a call light goes off. And it’s like my brain just drops the whole thread. I’ll suddenly be standing there thinking, “Wait… what was I doing?” and I have to backtrack through the last few minutes to reconstruct it.\n\nSo with things I enjoy in my free time… I’m not sure I can give you as clear of an answer right now. It’s not what made me come in. The reason I’m here is really that at work, with all those interruptions, I lose my train of thought constantly, and it’s been really frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble staying focused when you're doing quiet activities alone?\n\nPatient: I know you’re asking specifically about quiet activities alone, and I’m trying to answer that directly, but I keep coming back to the same thing because that’s honestly what feels like the problem I can actually describe clearly.\n\nThe main reason I’m here is that on the floor, I lose my train of thought constantly. I’ll be holding a patient flow issue in my head — like what I was about to follow up on, who needed something next, what step I was in the middle of — and then something pretty normal happens, like an IV pump beeping, or someone asks another nurse a question nearby, or a call light goes off. And suddenly it’s just gone. Like I’ve forgotten what I was working on, and I have to stand there and mentally rewind.\n\nSo for quiet activities alone… I don’t have as clear of an example right now. It’s really the work situation, with all the interruptions, that made me feel like, okay, this is happening constantly and it’s becoming a real problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience difficulty staying focused or losing your train of thought when you are alone at home?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I’m still having a hard time giving you a clean answer about being alone at home. I don’t want to say something just to make it fit if that’s not really what stands out to me.\n\nWhat I can say is the main thing that made me come in is what happens on the floor. That’s where I notice it constantly. I’ll be trying to hold a patient flow issue in my head—like who I was about to check on, what needed follow-up, what step I was in the middle of—and then an IV pump beeps, or someone nearby asks another nurse a question, or a call light goes off, and it’s like the whole thread just disappears. I’ll literally stop and think, “Wait, what was I just doing?” and then I have to reconstruct it.\n\nSo at home alone… I’m not saying it never happens, I just don’t have a clear example the way I do at work. The work interruptions are the part that feels constant and really disruptive.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have trouble staying focused if you were doing a complex task at home without any interruptions?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would, actually. Maybe not in the same way as when there are constant interruptions, but if the task itself requires a lot of sustained focus, I really struggle to even get myself to start it.\n\nLike, if I know something is going to involve sitting down and doing a detailed chart review—say auditing documentation across shifts or going back through notes carefully and comparing things—I’ll keep putting it off. I’ll tell myself, “Okay, I need to do that,” and then somehow I’m suddenly doing every bedside task I can justify doing first. Checking on something, restocking something, answering something, tidying up, anything that feels more immediate and concrete.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t understand the task. It’s more like sitting down with it feels like trying to climb a wall. I can feel this resistance before I even start, because I know it’s going to take that steady mental effort and I’m going to have to hold all these details in my head. So if I were at home with a complex task like that, even without interruptions, I think the difficulty would still be there—especially if it was the kind of thing that takes prolonged, focused review.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be more able to focus on a complex task at home if it was something you were really interested in?\n\nPatient: Maybe somewhat, but I don’t think interest completely fixes it for me. Like, if something is genuinely engaging, I might have an easier time getting into it, at least at first. But the part I keep noticing is when I know a task is going to take that heavy, sustained mental effort — especially something like focused chart review or auditing documentation across shifts — I start avoiding it.\n\nIt’s like I can feel the weight of it before I even begin. I’ll think, “Okay, I need to sit down and go through this carefully,” and then I’ll suddenly find ten bedside tasks that feel more concrete or urgent. I’ll check on something, organize something, follow up on something smaller, because those feel easier to move through. But actually sitting down and holding all those details in my head feels like trying to climb a wall.\n\nSo if it were something I was really interested in at home, I might be more willing to start, but if it still required that same kind of prolonged, detailed focus, I think I’d still struggle and probably push it off.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with staying focused is related to not being motivated or interested in the tasks you're doing?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s just motivation or interest, no. I mean, interest probably helps a little, like if something feels more engaging I can sometimes get myself into it easier. But the problem is bigger than just, “I don’t care about this.”\n\nThe clearest example is when I know something is going to take a lot of sustained focus, like really sitting down to do chart review or auditing documentation across shifts. I know it matters, and I’m not indifferent to it. I can even feel stressed because I know it needs to get done. But I keep pushing it off anyway. I’ll suddenly find all these bedside tasks that feel more immediate or concrete — checking on something, following up on something smaller, organizing something, anything where I can keep moving.\n\nAnd it’s not like I’m choosing fun things instead. That’s the part that makes me think it’s not just motivation. I’m not avoiding work to relax. I’m avoiding the kind of work where I have to sit still mentally and hold a bunch of details in my head for a long stretch. Sitting down with that kind of task feels like trying to climb a wall, even when I know it’s important.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble staying focused when doing tasks in a quiet, private space without interruptions?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually one of the things that makes it frustrating, because I can’t always blame it on noise or people interrupting me.\n\nIf I’m in a quiet room and there’s nothing obviously pulling me away, I still have trouble with tasks that require sustained mental effort. Like if I know I need to sit down and do a focused chart review, or audit documentation across shifts, I’ll keep avoiding it. I’ll tell myself, “Okay, now is the time, I’m alone, it’s quiet, I should be able to just do this,” and then I still somehow find other things to do first.\n\nAt work, that usually looks like me getting up to do bedside tasks instead — checking on something, organizing something, following up on something smaller — because those feel more concrete and easier to start. The chart review feels like this big wall in front of me. It’s not even that I don’t know how to do it. It’s more that sitting down and staying with it, holding all the details in my head and going through them carefully, feels almost impossible to make myself do.\n\nSo yes, even without interruptions, if the task is mentally heavy and detailed, I really struggle to stay focused on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it easier to focus when you are in a familiar environment, like your home, compared to a busy workplace?\n\nPatient: Honestly, the familiar environment helps a little, but the bigger issue for me is how busy and distracting the floor is. That’s actually one of the main reasons I’m here.\n\nAt work, I lose my train of thought constantly. I’ll be in the middle of thinking through some patient flow issue — like what needs to happen next, who still needs something, what I was about to follow up on — and then an IV pump starts beeping, or someone asks another nurse a question near me, or a call light goes off, and it’s like the thought just disappears. I’ll stop and think, “Wait, what was I doing?” or “What was I about to check?” And sometimes I can get it back, but other times I have to retrace everything mentally, which takes time and makes me feel really scattered.\n\nSo compared to that, yes, home can feel easier because there aren’t as many constant alarms and voices and interruptions. But it’s not like being somewhere familiar magically makes my focus solid. It’s more that the workplace has so many competing things pulling at my attention that I’m constantly getting knocked off track.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your difficulty staying focused is the same whether you're doing work tasks or personal tasks?\n\nPatient: It’s not exactly the same, no. I mean, I can get distracted with personal things too, but the place where it’s really obvious — and honestly the main reason I’m here — is at work on the floor.\n\nWhen I’m doing work tasks, especially when I’m trying to keep track of patient flow or what needs to happen next, I lose my train of thought constantly. I’ll be in the middle of thinking, “Okay, this patient needs this, I need to follow up on that, I was about to check—” and then an IV pump starts beeping, or someone nearby asks another nurse a question, or a call light goes off, and suddenly the whole thread is just gone. Like completely gone. I’ll stand there thinking, “Wait, what was I doing?” or “Who was I about to go check on?”\n\nAnd it’s frustrating because it’s not like I’m trying to drift off. I’m actively working, I’m trying to stay on top of things, but the interruptions or even just sounds around me knock me off course so easily. Then I have to backtrack in my head and reconstruct what I was doing, which takes extra time and makes me feel scattered.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say it’s only work tasks, but work is where it causes the biggest problems, because there are so many alarms and voices and things happening all at once.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have the same difficulty staying focused if you were doing your nursing tasks in a quieter, less busy environment?\n\nPatient: I think it would probably be better in a quieter environment, but I don’t know that it would completely go away.\n\nThe main thing that’s making me come in is that on the floor, I lose my train of thought constantly. I’ll be in the middle of sorting out some patient flow issue in my head — like who needs what next, what I was about to follow up on, what step I was going to take — and then an IV pump beeps, or someone nearby asks another nurse a question, or a call light goes off, and it’s like the whole thought just disappears. I’m left standing there thinking, “Wait, what was I just doing?” or “Which patient was I thinking about?”\n\nSo if the environment were quieter and less busy, I do think fewer things would be knocking me off track. But the reason it feels so bad at work is that there are just constant little interruptions and sounds, and each one can completely derail me. It’s not even always someone directly interrupting me — sometimes it’s just something happening nearby, and my brain follows it before I can stop it.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble staying focused when interacting with family or friends?\n\nPatient: With family or friends, I can get distracted sometimes, but it’s honestly not the thing that stands out most to me. The main reason I’m here is really what happens at work, on the floor.\n\nLike, I’ll be in the middle of thinking through a patient flow issue — who needs what next, what I was about to follow up on, what I still need to check — and then an IV pump starts beeping, or someone near me asks another nurse a question, or a call light goes off, and my whole train of thought just drops. Suddenly I’m standing there like, “Wait, what was I doing?” or “Which patient was I just thinking about?” It feels like the thought gets erased.\n\nSo with family or friends, it’s not that I’m never distracted, but it doesn’t feel as disruptive or as obvious as it does at work. On the floor, there are just so many sounds and interruptions that I’m constantly getting pulled away from what I was trying to hold in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you are supposed to be sitting still?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not really sure, honestly. I don’t think of myself as especially fidgety, at least not in the obvious way, like constantly bouncing my leg or needing to get up all the time. I mean, I’m sure I shift around like anyone does if I’m sitting for a long time, but I haven’t really noticed that as a major problem.\n\nWhen I’m supposed to be sitting still, I don’t usually feel like I physically can’t tolerate it or anything. It’s more that I might get uncomfortable or impatient depending on the situation, but I don’t know if that’s more than normal. No one has really pointed it out to me either, like, “You’re always moving,” or anything like that.\n\nSo I guess my answer would be… not often, or at least it’s not something I’ve paid much attention to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a major way. I mean, I’m trying to think of what would count as impulsive. Like, if you mean blurting things out, making risky decisions, spending a bunch of money suddenly, or doing things where afterward I’m like, “Why did I do that?” — that’s not really something I see as a big pattern for me.\n\nI can be reactive sometimes, like if I’m stressed or overwhelmed I might answer too quickly or feel like I want to just get something done and move on, but I don’t usually act without any thought about consequences. I’m pretty aware of consequences, maybe even overly aware sometimes. Especially with work and responsibilities, I tend to think about what could go wrong.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say I often act on impulse. At least it’s not one of the problems that stands out to me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Then I have to backtrack in my head and reconstruct what I was doing, which takes extra time and makes me feel scattered.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say it’s only work tasks, but work is where it causes the biggest problems, because there are so many alarms and voices and things happening all at once.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have the same difficulty staying focused if you were doing your nursing tasks in a quieter, less busy environment?\n\nPatient: I think it would probably be better in a quieter environment, but I don’t know that it would completely go away.\n\nThe main thing that’s making me come in is that on the floor, I lose my train of thought constantly. I’ll be in the middle of sorting out some patient flow issue in my head — like who needs what next, what I was about to follow up on, what step I was going to take — and then an IV pump beeps, or someone nearby asks another nurse a question, or a call light goes off, and it’s like the whole thought just disappears. I’m left standing there thinking, “Wait, what was I just doing?” or “Which patient was I thinking about?”\n\nSo if the environment were quieter and less busy, I do think fewer things would be knocking me off track. I don’t think so, not really. I mean, I’m trying to picture situations where I’m supposed to be sitting still, like meetings, trainings, sitting through an appointment, that kind of thing. I don’t usually have this strong feeling like I need to jump up or pace around or leave my seat.

Sitting down with that kind of task feels like trying to climb a wall, even when I know it’s important.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble staying focused when doing tasks in a quiet, private space without interruptions?\n\nPatient: Yes. Like if I know I need to sit down and do a focused chart review, or audit documentation across shifts, I’ll keep avoiding it. I’ll tell myself, “Okay, now is the time, I’m alone, it’s quiet, I should be able to just do this,” and then I still somehow find other things to do first.\n\nAt work, that usually looks like me getting up to do bedside tasks instead — checking on something, organizing something, following up on something smaller — because those feel more concrete and easier to start. The chart review feels like this big wall in front of me. It’s not even that I don’t know how to do it. It’s more that sitting down and staying with it, holding all the details in my head and going through them carefully, feels almost impossible to make myself do.\n\nSo yes, even without interruptions, if the task is mentally heavy and detailed, I really struggle to stay focused on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it easier to focus when you are in a familiar environment, like your home, compared to a busy workplace?\n\nPatient: Honestly, the familiar environment helps a little, but the bigger issue for me is how busy and distracting the floor is. That’s actually one of the main reasons I’m here.\n\nAt work, I lose my train of thought constantly. I’ll be in the middle of thinking through some patient flow issue — like what needs to happen next, who still needs something, what I was about to follow up on — and then an IV pump starts beeping, or someone asks another nurse a question near me, or a call light goes off, and it’s like the thought just disappears. I’ll stop and think, “Wait, what was I doing?” or “What was I about to check?” And sometimes I can get it back, but other times I have to retrace everything mentally, which takes time and makes me feel really scattered.\n\nSo compared to that, yes, home can feel easier because there aren’t as many constant alarms and voices and interruptions. But it’s not like being somewhere familiar magically makes my focus solid. It’s more that the workplace has so many competing things pulling at my attention that I’m constantly getting knocked off track.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your difficulty staying focused is the same whether you're doing work tasks or personal tasks?\n\nPatient: It’s not exactly the same, no. I mean, I can get distracted with personal things too, but the place where it’s really obvious — and honestly the main reason I’m here — is at work on the floor.\n\nWhen I’m doing work tasks, especially when I’m trying to keep track of patient flow or what needs to happen next, I lose my train of thought constantly. I’ll be in the middle of thinking, “Okay, this patient needs this, I need to follow up on that, I was about to check—” and then an IV pump starts beeping, or someone nearby asks another nurse a question, or a call light goes off, and suddenly the whole thread is just gone. Like completely gone. I’ll stand there thinking, “Wait, what was I doing?” or “Who was I about to go check on?”\n\nAnd it’s frustrating because it’s not like I’m trying to drift off. I’m actively working, I’m trying to stay on top of things, but the interruptions or even just sounds around me knock me off course so easily. Then I have to backtrack in my head and reconstruct what I was doing, which takes extra time and makes me feel scattered.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say it’s only work tasks, but work is where it causes the biggest problems, because there are so many alarms and voices and things happening all at once.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have the same difficulty staying focused if you were doing your nursing tasks in a quieter, less busy environment?\n\nPatient: I think it would probably be better in a quieter environment, but I don’t know that it would completely go away.\n\nThe main thing that’s making me come in is that on the floor, I lose my train of thought constantly. I’ll be in the middle of sorting out some patient flow issue in my head — like who needs what next, what I was about to follow up on, what step I was going to take — and then an IV pump beeps, or someone nearby asks another nurse a question, or a call light goes off, and it’s like the whole thought just disappears. I’m left standing there thinking, “Wait, what was I just doing?” or “Which patient was I thinking about?”\n\nSo if the environment were quieter and less busy, I do think fewer things would be knocking me off track. But the reason it feels so bad at work is that there are just constant little interruptions and sounds, and each one can completely derail me. It’s not even always someone directly interrupting me — sometimes it’s just something happening nearby, and my brain follows it before I can stop it.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble staying focused when interacting with family or friends?\n\nPatient: With family or friends, I can get distracted sometimes, but it’s honestly not the thing that stands out most to me. The main reason I’m here is really what happens at work, on the floor.\n\nLike, I’ll be in the middle of thinking through a patient flow issue — who needs what next, what I was about to follow up on, what I still need to check — and then an IV pump starts beeping, or someone near me asks another nurse a question, or a call light goes off, and my whole train of thought just drops. Suddenly I’m standing there like, “Wait, what was I doing?” or “Which patient was I just thinking about?” It feels like the thought gets erased.\n\nSo with family or friends, it’s not that I’m never distracted, but it doesn’t feel as disruptive or as obvious as it does at work. On the floor, there are just so many sounds and interruptions that I’m constantly getting pulled away from what I was trying to hold in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you are supposed to be sitting still?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not really sure, honestly. I don’t think of myself as especially fidgety, at least not in the obvious way, like constantly bouncing my leg or needing to get up all the time. I mean, I’m sure I shift around like anyone does if I’m sitting for a long time, but I haven’t really noticed that as a major problem.\n\nWhen I’m supposed to be sitting still, I don’t usually feel like I physically can’t tolerate it or anything. It’s more that I might get uncomfortable or impatient depending on the situation, but I don’t know if that’s more than normal. No one has really pointed it out to me either, like, “You’re always moving,” or anything like that.\n\nSo I guess my answer would be… not often, or at least it’s not something I’ve paid much attention to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a major way. I mean, I’m trying to think of what would count as impulsive. Like, if you mean blurting things out, making risky decisions, spending a bunch of money suddenly, or doing things where afterward I’m like, “Why did I do that?” — that’s not really something I see as a big pattern for me.\n\nI can be reactive sometimes, like if I’m stressed or overwhelmed I might answer too quickly or feel like I want to just get something done and move on, but I don’t usually act without any thought about consequences. I’m pretty aware of consequences, maybe even overly aware sometimes. Especially with work and responsibilities, I tend to think about what could go wrong.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say I often act on impulse. It’s not one of the things that really stands out to me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Right, I know this is similar to what you were just asking, and I’m trying not to overthink the wording. I wouldn’t say that’s something I often experience, at least not in the physical sense.

Like, if I’m in a meeting or sitting through something where I’m expected to stay seated, I can usually do that. I might feel bored or mentally checked out if it goes on too long, or I might be waiting for it to be over, but I don’t usually have this strong restless feeling where I’m fighting the urge to stand up or walk around.

I guess I can get uncomfortable like anyone does, and I’ll shift in my chair or change positions, but it doesn’t feel like a major issue. No one has really commented that I can’t sit still, and I don’t think I’m someone who’s constantly getting up when I shouldn’t.

So… no, I don’t think so. Not often, anyway.

Yes, definitely. I mean, that’s actually kind of what makes this confusing for me, because if I’m following a really clear routine or a checklist, I can usually stay with it much better. Like if there’s a set sequence and I can almost let the structure carry me, I’m okay.

I’ll stop and think, “Wait, what was I just doing?” and sometimes I have to backtrack mentally through the last few minutes to remember where I was. It’s happening enough that it’s become the main reason I thought I should come talk to someone, because the floor is already busy and full of interruptions, but I feel like my brain is getting pulled off course way too easily.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing difficulties with focus or staying on track in situations outside of your job as a nurse?\n\nPatient: I mean… the clearest place I notice it is definitely at work, because the floor is just so full of interruptions and consequences, you know? That’s honestly the main reason I came in. I’ll be trying to hold a patient flow issue in my head—like who needs what next, what I was about to follow up on, what’s pending—and then an IV pump beeps, or someone nearby asks another nurse something, or a call light goes off, and it’s like the whole thought just drops out of my brain.\n\nSo outside work… I’m not sure I can describe it as specifically right now, but the work piece is constant and really hard to ignore. It’s that losing-my-train-of-thought thing that’s making me feel like something is off, because I’ll suddenly be standing there thinking, “Wait, what was I doing?” and then I have to kind of retrace my steps mentally. It happens so much on the floor that it’s become really frustrating and embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble staying focused or getting distracted when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: I… honestly, I know you’re asking about outside of work, and I don’t want to dodge the question, but the place it’s most obvious—and the reason I came in—is really the floor. That’s where it feels almost constant.\n\nLike I’ll be in the middle of thinking through a patient flow issue, trying to keep track of what I was about to do next, and then an IV pump beeps, or someone nearby asks another nurse something, or a call light goes off, and suddenly I’ve lost the whole thread. It’s not just that I’m momentarily interrupted—it’s like I genuinely forget what I was working on, and I’m standing there trying to reconstruct it in my head.\n\nOutside of work, I’m not sure I can give as clear an example right now, but at work it happens so often that it’s really what made me feel like I needed help.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any difficulty staying focused when you are doing things you enjoy in your free time?\n\nPatient: Um… I know you’re asking about free time, and I’m trying to think of a good example, but honestly the thing that’s most glaring to me is still work. That’s where it feels almost impossible to ignore.\n\nLike on the floor, I’ll be in the middle of working through a patient flow issue in my head — who I was going to check on next, what needed follow-up, what I was about to ask someone — and then an IV pump starts beeping, or someone asks another nurse a question near me, or a call light goes off. And it’s like my brain just drops the whole thread. I’ll suddenly be standing there thinking, “Wait… what was I doing?” and I have to backtrack through the last few minutes to reconstruct it.\n\nSo with things I enjoy in my free time… I’m not sure I can give you as clear of an answer right now. It’s not what made me come in. The reason I’m here is really that at work, with all those interruptions, I lose my train of thought constantly, and it’s been really frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble staying focused when you're doing quiet activities alone?\n\nPatient: I know you’re asking specifically about quiet activities alone, and I’m trying to answer that directly, but I keep coming back to the same thing because that’s honestly what feels like the problem I can actually describe clearly.\n\nThe main reason I’m here is that on the floor, I lose my train of thought constantly. I’ll be holding a patient flow issue in my head — like what I was about to follow up on, who needed something next, what step I was in the middle of — and then something pretty normal happens, like an IV pump beeping, or someone asks another nurse a question nearby, or a call light goes off. And suddenly it’s just gone. Like I’ve forgotten what I was working on, and I have to stand there and mentally rewind.\n\nSo for quiet activities alone… I don’t have as clear of an example right now. It’s really the work situation, with all the interruptions, that made me feel like, okay, this is happening constantly and it’s becoming a real problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience difficulty staying focused or losing your train of thought when you are alone at home?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I’m still having a hard time giving you a clean answer about being alone at home. I don’t want to say something just to make it fit if that’s not really what stands out to me.\n\nWhat I can say is the main thing that made me come in is what happens on the floor. That’s where I notice it constantly. I’ll be trying to hold a patient flow issue in my head—like who I was about to check on, what needed follow-up, what step I was in the middle of—and then an IV pump beeps, or someone nearby asks another nurse a question, or a call light goes off, and it’s like the whole thread just disappears. I’ll literally stop and think, “Wait, what was I just doing?” and then I have to reconstruct it.\n\nSo at home alone… I’m not saying it never happens, I just don’t have a clear example the way I do at work. The work interruptions are the part that feels constant and really disruptive.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have trouble staying focused if you were doing a complex task at home without any interruptions?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would, actually. Maybe not in the same way as when there are constant interruptions, but if the task itself requires a lot of sustained focus, I really struggle to even get myself to start it.\n\nLike, if I know something is going to involve sitting down and doing a detailed chart review—say auditing documentation across shifts or going back through notes carefully and comparing things—I’ll keep putting it off. I’ll tell myself, “Okay, I need to do that,” and then somehow I’m suddenly doing every bedside task I can justify doing first. Checking on something, restocking something, answering something, tidying up, anything that feels more immediate and concrete.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t understand the task. It’s more like sitting down with it feels like trying to climb a wall. I can feel this resistance before I even start, because I know it’s going to take that steady mental effort and I’m going to have to hold all these details in my head. So if I were at home with a complex task like that, even without interruptions, I think the difficulty would still be there—especially if it was the kind of thing that takes prolonged, focused review.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be more able to focus on a complex task at home if it was something you were really interested in?\n\nPatient: Maybe somewhat, but I don’t think interest completely fixes it for me. Like, if something is genuinely engaging, I might have an easier time getting into it, at least at first. But the part I keep noticing is when I know a task is going to take that heavy, sustained mental effort — especially something like focused chart review or auditing documentation across shifts — I start avoiding it.\n\nIt’s like I can feel the weight of it before I even begin. I’ll think, “Okay, I need to sit down and go through this carefully,” and then I’ll suddenly find ten bedside tasks that feel more concrete or urgent. I’ll check on something, organize something, follow up on something smaller, because those feel easier to move through. But actually sitting down and holding all those details in my head feels like trying to climb a wall.\n\nSo if it were something I was really interested in at home, I might be more willing to start, but if it still required that same kind of prolonged, detailed focus, I think I’d still struggle and probably push it off.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with staying focused is related to not being motivated or interested in the tasks you're doing?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s just motivation or interest, no. I mean, interest probably helps a little, like if something feels more engaging I can sometimes get myself into it easier. But the problem is bigger than just, “I don’t care about this.”\n\nThe clearest example is when I know something is going to take a lot of sustained focus, like really sitting down to do chart review or auditing documentation across shifts. I know it matters, and I’m not indifferent to it. I can even feel stressed because I know it needs to get done. But I keep pushing it off anyway. I’ll suddenly find all these bedside tasks that feel more immediate or concrete — checking on something, following up on something smaller, organizing something, anything where I can keep moving.\n\nAnd it’s not like I’m choosing fun things instead. That’s the part that makes me think it’s not just motivation. I’m not avoiding work to relax. I’m avoiding the kind of work where I have to sit still mentally and hold a bunch of details in my head for a long stretch. Sitting down with that kind of task feels like trying to climb a wall, even when I know it’s important.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble staying focused when doing tasks in a quiet, private space without interruptions?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually one of the things that makes it frustrating, because I can’t always blame it on noise or people interrupting me.\n\nIf I’m in a quiet room and there’s nothing obviously pulling me away, I still have trouble with tasks that require sustained mental effort. Like if I know I need to sit down and do a focused chart review, or audit documentation across shifts, I’ll keep avoiding it. I’ll tell myself, “Okay, now is the time, I’m alone, it’s quiet, I should be able to just do this,” and then I still somehow find other things to do first.\n\nAt work, that usually looks like me getting up to do bedside tasks instead — checking on something, organizing something, following up on something smaller — because those feel more concrete and easier to start. The chart review feels like this big wall in front of me. It’s not even that I don’t know how to do it. It’s more that sitting down and staying with it, holding all the details in my head and going through them carefully, feels almost impossible to make myself do.\n\nSo yes, even without interruptions, if the task is mentally heavy and detailed, I really struggle to stay focused on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it easier to focus when you are in a familiar environment, like your home, compared to a busy workplace?\n\nPatient: Honestly, the familiar environment helps a little, but the bigger issue for me is how busy and distracting the floor is. That’s actually one of the main reasons I’m here.\n\nAt work, I lose my train of thought constantly. I’ll be in the middle of thinking through some patient flow issue — like what needs to happen next, who still needs something, what I was about to follow up on — and then an IV pump starts beeping, or someone asks another nurse a question near me, or a call light goes off, and it’s like the thought just disappears. I’ll stop and think, “Wait, what was I doing?” or “What was I about to check?” And sometimes I can get it back, but other times I have to retrace everything mentally, which takes time and makes me feel really scattered.\n\nSo compared to that, yes, home can feel easier because there aren’t as many constant alarms and voices and interruptions. But it’s not like being somewhere familiar magically makes my focus solid. It’s more that the workplace has so many competing things pulling at my attention that I’m constantly getting knocked off track.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your difficulty staying focused is the same whether you're doing work tasks or personal tasks?\n\nPatient: It’s not exactly the same, no. I mean, I can get distracted with personal things too, but the place where it’s really obvious — and honestly the main reason I’m here — is at work on the floor.\n\nWhen I’m doing work tasks, especially when I’m trying to keep track of patient flow or what needs to happen next, I lose my train of thought constantly. I’ll be in the middle of thinking, “Okay, this patient needs this, I need to follow up on that, I was about to check—” and then an IV pump starts beeping, or someone nearby asks another nurse a question, or a call light goes off, and suddenly the whole thread is just gone. Like completely gone. I’ll stand there thinking, “Wait, what was I doing?” or “Who was I about to go check on?”\n\nAnd it’s frustrating because it’s not like I’m trying to drift off. I’ll be in the middle of sorting out some patient flow issue in my head — like who needs what next, what I was about to follow up on, what step I was going to take — and then an IV pump beeps, or someone nearby asks another nurse a question, or a call light goes off, and it’s like the whole thought just disappears. I’m left standing there thinking, “Wait, what was I just doing?” or “Which patient was I thinking about?”\n\nSo if the environment were quieter and less busy, I do think fewer things would be knocking me off track. Suddenly I’m standing there like, “Wait, what was I doing?” or “Which patient was I just thinking about?” It feels like the thought gets erased.\n\nSo with family or friends, it’s not that I’m never distracted, but it doesn’t feel as disruptive or as obvious as it does at work. I don’t think of myself as especially fidgety, at least not in the obvious way, like constantly bouncing my leg or needing to get up all the time. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. Very much, honestly. That’s one of the biggest practical problems for me at work.

When I’m charge nurse, especially, I feel like I’m trying to hold way too many moving pieces in my head at once, so I start writing things down everywhere. I’ll have scraps of paper in my pocket, sticky notes at the desk, half-started lists on the report sheet, and then another list because I can’t find the first list or because the first one got too messy. It looks like I’m trying to be organized, but it doesn’t actually feel organized.

The hardest part is figuring out what needs to happen first. I can see that there are ten things that need attention, but I get stuck trying to sort the order, and then whatever is loudest or most immediate ends up taking over. Like if someone needs something right then, or there’s some issue that pops up, I’ll go handle that, and then by the time I come back I’ve lost track of what I was prioritizing before.

So the shift can turn into me putting out fires instead of managing the whole picture. And then near the end, I’m suddenly rushing because things I meant to do earlier are still sitting there, or I realize I didn’t follow through on something in the order I should have. It’s really stressful because I’m working the whole time — it’s not like I’m sitting around doing nothing — but the time still gets away from me.

I’m trying really hard, actually, which is part of why it’s so frustrating. I’ll stop and think, “Wait, what was I just doing?” and sometimes I have to backtrack mentally through the last few minutes to remember where I was. It’s happening enough that it’s become the main reason I thought I should come talk to someone, because the floor is already busy and full of interruptions, but I feel like my brain is getting pulled off course way too easily.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing difficulties with focus or staying on track in situations outside of your job as a nurse?\n\nPatient: I mean… the clearest place I notice it is definitely at work, because the floor is just so full of interruptions and consequences, you know? That’s honestly the main reason I came in. I’ll be trying to hold a patient flow issue in my head—like who needs what next, what I was about to follow up on, what’s pending—and then an IV pump beeps, or someone nearby asks another nurse something, or a call light goes off, and it’s like the whole thought just drops out of my brain.\n\nSo outside work… I’m not sure I can describe it as specifically right now, but the work piece is constant and really hard to ignore. That’s where it feels almost constant.\n\nLike I’ll be in the middle of thinking through a patient flow issue, trying to keep track of what I was about to do next, and then an IV pump beeps, or someone nearby asks another nurse something, or a call light goes off, and suddenly I’ve lost the whole thread. It’s not just that I’m momentarily interrupted—it’s like I genuinely forget what I was working on, and I’m standing there trying to reconstruct it in my head.\n\nOutside of work, I’m not sure I can give as clear an example right now, but at work it happens so often that it’s really what made me feel like I needed help.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any difficulty staying focused when you are doing things you enjoy in your free time?\n\nPatient: Um… I know you’re asking about free time, and I’m trying to think of a good example, but honestly the thing that’s most glaring to me is still work. The reason I’m here is really that at work, with all those interruptions, I lose my train of thought constantly, and it’s been really frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble staying focused when you're doing quiet activities alone?\n\nPatient: I know you’re asking specifically about quiet activities alone, and I’m trying to answer that directly, but I keep coming back to the same thing because that’s honestly what feels like the problem I can actually describe clearly.\n\nThe main reason I’m here is that on the floor, I lose my train of thought constantly. I’ll be holding a patient flow issue in my head — like what I was about to follow up on, who needed something next, what step I was in the middle of — and then something pretty normal happens, like an IV pump beeping, or someone asks another nurse a question nearby, or a call light goes off. And suddenly it’s just gone. Like I’ve forgotten what I was working on, and I have to stand there and mentally rewind.\n\nSo for quiet activities alone… I don’t have as clear of an example right now. It’s really the work situation, with all the interruptions, that made me feel like, okay, this is happening constantly and it’s becoming a real problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience difficulty staying focused or losing your train of thought when you are alone at home?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I’m still having a hard time giving you a clean answer about being alone at home. I don’t want to say something just to make it fit if that’s not really what stands out to me.\n\nWhat I can say is the main thing that made me come in is what happens on the floor. That’s where I notice it constantly. I’ll be trying to hold a patient flow issue in my head—like who I was about to check on, what needed follow-up, what step I was in the middle of—and then an IV pump beeps, or someone nearby asks another nurse a question, or a call light goes off, and it’s like the whole thread just disappears.
